# Plan: Blueprint Documentation + Import Links

## Context

Three HA blueprints were created in PR #902 at `blueprints/` in the repo root. Users need documentation and easy import links. No programmatic installation — just docs.

Wiki changes will be staged but **not committed** until the blueprint PR is released.

## 1. New Wiki Page: `Blueprints.md`

Three blueprints span different features, so a dedicated page with My HA import badges for each:

```
# Blueprints

Intro: LCM provides blueprints for common automation patterns.
Import them using the buttons below, or copy the YAML files from
the blueprints/ directory in the repository.

## Slot Usage Limiter (automation)
- [Import badge]
- What it does, setup steps (create input_number, import, configure)
- Inputs table

## Calendar Condition (template)
- [Import badge]
- What it does, setup, assign binary_sensor as condition entity
- Inputs table
- Available template variables list
- Note about condition_template evaluation timing

## Calendar PIN Setter (automation)
- [Import badge]
- What it does, setup steps
- Inputs table
- Example PIN templates
```

Import badge format:
```markdown
[![Import](https://my.home-assistant.io/badges/blueprint_import.svg)](https://my.home-assistant.io/redirect/blueprint_import/?blueprint_url=https%3A%2F%2Fgithub.com%2Framan325%2Flock_code_manager%2Fblob%2Fmain%2Fblueprints%2Fautomation%2Flock_code_manager%2Fslot_usage_limiter.yaml)
```

     1→# Lock Code Manager
     2→
     3→Lock Code Manager is a Home Assistant integration that allows you to more easily manage
     4→your usercodes on your locks. Once you have configured it, the integration will set and
     5→clear codes on your locks as needed depending on how you decide to configure them.
     6→
     7→Features:
     8→
     9→- Synchronize multiple locks with a single set of codes
    10→- Optionally use a condition entity to control when a code is active. Supported entity types:
    11→  - `calendar` - code is active when an event is in progress
    12→  - `binary_sensor` - code is active when the sensor is `on`
    13→  - `switch` - code is active when the switch is `on`
    14→  - `schedule` - code is active when the schedule is `on`
    15→  - `input_boolean` - code is active when the input boolean is `on`
    16→- Optionally define a maximum number of uses for a code before the code is disabled
    17→
    18→Locks from the following integrations are currently supported:
    19→
    20→- Z-Wave
    21→- [Virtual](https://github.com/twrecked/hass-virtual) custom integration. See the
    22→  [Wiki page on this integration](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/wiki/Virtual-integration)
    23→  for more details on why it was built and how it works.
    24→
    25→The code was written to make it (I think) easy to add support for locks in other
    26→integrations. Check the [Wiki](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/wiki) if you
    27→want to learn more about that and take a stab at it. Contributors welcome!
    28→
    29→## Integrations That Cannot Currently Be Supported
    30→
    31→Many lock integrations don't yet have Lock Code Manager providers, but could potentially be
    32→added in the future. The integrations listed below are different - they **cannot** be
    33→supported due to fundamental limitations in their underlying libraries or protocols.
    34→
    35→The Home Assistant lock platform only requires lock/unlock operations. User code management
    36→is optional, and these integrations don't expose it.
    37→
    38→| Integration | Reason |
    39→| ----------- | ------ |
    40→| **ESPHome** | The ESPHome lock component only supports lock/unlock/open commands. User code management would need to be implemented in ESPHome's firmware and API first. |
    41→| **Yale/August** (`august`, `yale`, `yalexs_ble`, `yale_smart_alarm`) | The underlying libraries have limited support. The `yalexs` library (used by both `august` and `yale`) can read PINs via `async_get_pins()` but has no methods to create, update, or delete them. The `yalexs-ble` and `yalesmartalarmclient` libraries don't expose any PIN management. Support would require upstream library changes. |
    42→
    43→If you're interested in adding support for one of these integrations, the blocker is
    44→typically the underlying Python library - not Lock Code Manager itself. Contributing user
    45→code management features to those libraries would enable LCM support.
    46→
    47→## Condition Entity Integrations Not Supported
    48→
    49→Some integrations create switch or binary_sensor entities that appear compatible but have
    50→semantics that don't map to Lock Code Manager's access control model:
    51→
    52→| Integration | Reason |
    53→| ----------- | ------ |
    54→| **[scheduler-component](https://github.com/nielsfaber/scheduler-component)** | Creates switch entities for task scheduling, not time-based access. The `on` state means "schedule is enabled and waiting," not "access is currently allowed." Use the native [Schedule helper](https://www.home-assistant.io/integrations/schedule/) instead. |
    55→
    56→See the [wiki](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/wiki/Unsupported-Condition-Entity-Integrations) for details.
    57→
    58→## Installation
    59→
    60→The best way to install this integration is via HACS.
    61→
    62→1. Set up your locks as entities to your Home Assistant instance through the corresponding
    63→   integration (e.g. Z-Wave)
    64→2. Add this repository as a custom integration repository in HACS
    65→3. Go to Settings > Devices & Services > Add Integration
    66→4. Select Lock Code Manager
    67→5. Follow the prompts - additional information about the configuration options are
    68→   available in the Wiki
    69→
    70→## Learn More
    71→
    72→The [Wiki](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/wiki) is a WIP but has some
    73→content that might be helpful for you!
    74→
    75→## UI & Dashboards
    76→
    77→Lock Code Manager provides Lovelace strategies and custom cards for managing and monitoring
    78→PINs. You can use the dashboard strategy (full dashboard), the view strategy (single view
    79→for a config entry), or the custom cards directly.
    80→
    81→See [DASHBOARD_CONFIGURATION.md](DASHBOARD_CONFIGURATION.md) for detailed configuration
    82→options, or the [wiki](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/wiki/Add-a-UI-for-lock-code-management#dashboard)
    83→for additional guidance.
    84→
    85→## Inspiration
    86→
    87→I spent some time contributing to [keymaster](https://github.com/FutureTense/keymaster),
    88→and what I learned working on it, and the regular complaints users had about it generating
    89→too many automations, entities, etc. led me to take a different approach. This isn't a
    90→knock on `keymaster`, unfortunately a lot of what is built in this integration wasn't
    91→possible for most of `keymaster`'s life. I briefly considered implementing this into
    92→`keymaster` but:
    93→
    94→1. `keymaster` is still a great solution that works as is, and is more feature rich than
    95→   this integration will likely ever be.
    96→2. `keymaster` is surprisingly simple under the hood because it makes Home Assistant do a
    97→   lot of the heavy lifting for figuring out when to enable and disable a usercode. This
    98→   integration, on the other hand, attempts to do all of the heavy lifting internally in
    99→   code, which means it will generate less entities and automations but it is likely more
   100→   fragile to changes in HA Core or even changes in the codebase.
   101→3. It would be impossible to seamlessly migrate users from the current implementation of
   102→   `keymaster` to this integration's implementation. Rewriting `keymaster` to do this
   103→   would have been the equivalent of creating a new integration anyway, and since it's a
   104→   separate integration, users have a choice of what implementation they want to use.
   105→   Additionally, you can install the integrations side by side and slowly migrate your
   106→   locks over in either direction as needed.
   107→
   108→## Thanks
   109→
   110→A big thank you to the other `keymaster` maintainers:
   111→
   112→- @FutureTense
   113→- @firstof9
   114→
   115→As well as the person who created the base concept that `keymaster` evolved from:
   116→@ptdalen
   117→
```

     1→# Using Condition Entities
     2→
     3→Condition entities allow you to control when a PIN is active based on the state of another entity in Home Assistant. When a condition entity is configured for a slot, the PIN will only be active when:
     4→1. The slot is enabled
     5→2. Number of uses is disabled OR > 0
     6→3. **The condition entity state is `on`**
     7→
     8→## Supported Entity Types
     9→
    10→Lock Code Manager supports the following entity types as conditions:
    11→
    12→| Entity Type | Domain | Use Case |
    13→|------------|--------|----------|
    14→| **Calendar** | `calendar` | Schedule-based access with events (e.g., rental check-in/check-out times) |
    15→| **Schedule** | `schedule` | Simple recurring schedules (e.g., 9 AM - 5 PM weekdays) |
    16→| **Input Boolean** | `input_boolean` | Manual on/off toggle for access control |
    17→| **Switch** | `switch` | Control access via any switch entity |
    18→| **Binary Sensor** | `binary_sensor` | Control access via any binary sensor (great for template sensors!) |
    19→
    20→## Calendar Entities
    21→
    22→Calendars are ideal for non-recurring or complex schedules. The integration checks if there's an **ongoing event** at the current time.
    23→
    24→```yaml
    25→1:
    26→  pin: "1234"
    27→  entity_id: calendar.guest_access
    28→```
    29→
    30→**Features:**
    31→- Shows current event name and end time in the UI
    32→- Shows next upcoming event when no event is active
    33→- Create calendars using integrations like `local_calendar`
    34→
    35→**Tip:** You can use a single calendar across multiple slots, or create separate calendars per slot.
    36→
    37→## Schedule Entities
    38→
    39→[Schedule entities](https://www.home-assistant.io/integrations/schedule/) are perfect for simple recurring patterns like business hours.
    40→
    41→```yaml
    42→1:
    43→  pin: "1234"
    44→  entity_id: schedule.business_hours
    45→```
    46→
    47→**Features:**
    48→- Shows when the schedule will next turn on/off in the UI
    49→- Great for "every weekday 9-5" type schedules
    50→
    51→## Input Boolean / Switch Entities
    52→
    53→For manual control, use an input boolean or switch. Toggle it on to enable access, off to disable.
    54→
    55→```yaml
    56→1:
    57→  pin: "1234"
    58→  entity_id: input_boolean.guest_mode
    59→```
    60→
    61→## Binary Sensor Entities (Template Sensors)
    62→
    63→Binary sensors unlock the most flexibility. Use a [template binary sensor](https://www.home-assistant.io/integrations/template/) to create complex conditions:
    64→
    65→```yaml
    66→# Example: Only allow access when home is in guest mode AND it's daytime
    67→template:
    68→  - binary_sensor:
    69→      - name: "Guest Access Allowed"
    70→        state: >
    71→          {{ is_state('input_boolean.guest_mode', 'on')
    72→             and is_state('sun.sun', 'above_horizon') }}
    73→```
    74→
    75→Then reference it in your slot config:
    76→
    77→```yaml
    78→1:
    79→  pin: "1234"
    80→  entity_id: binary_sensor.guest_access_allowed
    81→```
    82→
    83→**Use cases:**
    84→- Combine multiple conditions (guest mode + time of day + presence)
    85→- Integrate with alarm systems (only allow access when disarmed)
    86→- Weather-based access (e.g., allow contractor access only on non-rainy days)
    87→
    88→## Useful Integrations for Creating Condition Entities
    89→
    90→These integrations are particularly useful for creating condition entities:
    91→
    92→| Integration | Description |
    93→| ----------- | ----------- |
    94→| **[Template](https://www.home-assistant.io/integrations/template/)** | Create binary sensors or switches with custom logic using Jinja2 templates. Great for combining multiple conditions. |
    95→| **[MQTT](https://www.home-assistant.io/integrations/mqtt/)** | Create entities controlled by external systems. Useful when access logic is managed outside Home Assistant. |
    96→| **[Group](https://www.home-assistant.io/integrations/group/)** | Combine multiple binary sensors into one. Access granted when all/any conditions are met. |
    97→| **[Workday](https://www.home-assistant.io/integrations/workday/)** | Binary sensor that's `on` on workdays (configurable for your country/region). |
    98→| **[Time of Day](https://www.home-assistant.io/integrations/tod/)** | Binary sensor based on time ranges, including sunrise/sunset offsets. |
    99→
   100→Know of another useful integration? Please [edit this wiki page](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/wiki/Using-Condition-Entities/_edit) to add it!
   101→
   102→**Note:** Some integrations create entities that appear compatible but have state semantics that don't work with LCM. See [[Unsupported Condition Entity Integrations|Unsupported-Condition-Entity-Integrations]] for details.
   103→
   104→## Migration from `calendar` (v1.0.0+)
   105→
   106→If you previously used the `calendar` configuration key, your configuration will be automatically migrated to use `entity_id`. No action is required.
   107→
   108→**Before (v0.9.x and earlier):**
   109→
   110→```yaml
   111→1:
   112→  pin: "1234"
   113→  calendar: calendar.my_schedule
   114→```
   115→
   116→**After (v1.0.0+):**
   117→
   118→```yaml
   119→1:
   120→  pin: "1234"
   121→  entity_id: calendar.my_schedule
   122→```
   123→
   124→## Limitations
   125→
   126→- **One condition entity per slot:** Each slot can only have one condition entity. For complex logic, use a template binary sensor to combine multiple conditions.
   127→- **State must be `on`:** Access is granted when the entity state is `on` (or has an active event for calendars). All other states block access.
   128→
```

     1→When "Number of Uses" is enabled for a slot, you can specify the number of times a PIN can be used to unlock a lock before the PIN is disabled. Note that the "Number of Uses" feature will only work if the lock(s) you are integrating with support tracking which PIN is used to unlock the door.

```
     1→---
     2→blueprint:
     3→  name: "Lock Code Manager: Slot Usage Limiter"
     4→  description: >
     5→    Decrements an `input_number` helper each time a code slot PIN is used.
     6→    When the counter reaches 0 the slot is automatically disabled.
     7→    Optionally resets the counter when the slot is re-enabled.
     8→
     9→
    10→    **Setup**
    11→
    12→    1. Create an `input_number` helper (Settings → Devices & Services → Helpers)
    13→       with min=0, max=your desired limit, and step=1.
    14→
    15→    2. Import this blueprint and configure it with your Lock Code Manager config
    16→       entry, slot number, and the helper you just created.
    17→
    18→
    19→    **Template debugging**
    20→
    21→    To test entity resolution, go to Developer Tools → Template and paste:
    22→
    23→    ```
    24→
    25→    {% set title = config_entry_attr('YOUR_CONFIG_ENTRY_ID', 'title') %}
    26→
    27→    {% set all = integration_entities(title) %}
    28→
    29→    {{ all | select('match', 'event\\..*_code_slot_1_pin_used') | list }}
    30→
    31→    ```
    32→
    33→    Replace YOUR_CONFIG_ENTRY_ID with your LCM config entry ID and 1 with your
    34→    slot number.
    35→  domain: automation
    36→  # yamllint disable-line rule:line-length
    37→  source_url: https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/blob/main/blueprints/automation/lock_code_manager/slot_usage_limiter.yaml
    38→  input:
    39→    config_entry:
    40→      name: Lock Code Manager config entry
    41→      description: The Lock Code Manager config entry that manages your lock(s).
    42→      selector:
    43→        config_entry:
    44→          integration: lock_code_manager
    45→    slot_number:
    46→      name: Code slot number
    47→      description: The code slot number to monitor.
    48→      selector:
    49→        number:
    50→          min: 1
    51→          max: 999
    52→          mode: box
    53→    uses_counter:
    54→      name: Uses counter
    55→      description: >
    56→        An `input_number` helper that tracks remaining uses.
    57→        Create one via Settings → Devices & Services → Helpers.
    58→      selector:
    59→        entity:
    60→          domain: input_number
    61→    initial_uses:
    62→      name: Initial uses on re-enable
    63→      description: >
    64→        When greater than 0, the counter is automatically reset to this value
    65→        each time the slot is re-enabled. Set to 0 to disable auto-reset.
    66→      default: 0
    67→      selector:
    68→        number:
    69→          min: 0
    70→          max: 999
    71→          mode: box
    72→
    73→mode: queued
    74→max: 10
    75→
    76→trigger_variables:
    77→  _config_entry: !input config_entry
    78→  _slot: !input slot_number
    79→  _title: >
    80→    {{ config_entry_attr(_config_entry, 'title') }}
    81→  _all_entities: >
    82→    {{ integration_entities(_title) }}
    83→  pin_used_entity: >
    84→    {{ _all_entities
    85→       | select('match', 'event\\..*_code_slot_' ~ _slot ~ '_pin_used')
    86→       | first | default('') }}
    87→  enabled_switch: >
    88→    {{ _all_entities
    89→       | select('match', 'switch\\..*_code_slot_' ~ _slot ~ '_enabled')
    90→       | first | default('') }}
    91→
    92→triggers:
    93→  - trigger: state
    94→    entity_id: "{{ pin_used_entity }}"
    95→    id: pin_used
    96→  - trigger: state
    97→    entity_id: "{{ enabled_switch }}"
    98→    id: slot_enabled
    99→    to: "on"
   100→
   101→variables:
   102→  initial_uses: !input initial_uses
   103→  uses_counter: !input uses_counter
   104→
   105→actions:
   106→  - choose:
   107→      - conditions:
   108→          - condition: trigger
   109→            id: pin_used
   110→        sequence:
   111→          - variables:
   112→              current: >
   113→                {{ states(uses_counter) | int(0) }}
   114→              new_count: >
   115→                {{ [current - 1, 0] | max }}
   116→          - action: input_number.set_value
   117→            target:
   118→              entity_id: !input uses_counter
   119→            data:
   120→              value: "{{ new_count }}"
   121→          - if:
   122→              - condition: template
   123→                value_template: "{{ new_count == 0 }}"
   124→            then:
   125→              - action: switch.turn_off
   126→                target:
   127→                  entity_id: "{{ enabled_switch }}"
   128→      - conditions:
   129→          - condition: trigger
   130→            id: slot_enabled
   131→          - condition: template
   132→            value_template: "{{ initial_uses | int(0) > 0 }}"
   133→        sequence:
   134→          - action: input_number.set_value
   135→            target:
   136→              entity_id: !input uses_counter
   137→            data:
   138→              value: !input initial_uses
   139→
```

     3→  name: "Lock Code Manager: Calendar PIN Setter"
     4→  description: >
     5→    Extracts a PIN from calendar event attributes using a user-provided template
     6→    and sets it on a Lock Code Manager code slot. Optionally clears the PIN when

    23→
    24→    - `all_day` — whether the event is an all-day event
    25→
    26→

    31→

    43→
    44→    {{ description | regex_findall('\\b\\d{4}\\b') | first | default('') }}

    54→      selector:
    55→        config_entry:
    56→          integration: lock_code_manager
    57→    slot_number:
    58→      name: Slot number
    59→      description: >
    60→        The code slot number. Can be a static number (e.g. `3`) or a Jinja2
    61→        template that resolves to a number using calendar event attributes
    62→        (e.g. `{{ description | regex_findall('slot (\d+)') | first }}`).
    63→      selector:
    64→        template:
    65→    calendar_entity:
    66→      name: Calendar entity
    67→      description: The calendar entity whose events provide PIN codes.
    68→      selector:
    69→        entity:
    70→          domain: calendar
    71→    pin_template:

    73→      description: >

    75→        Must evaluate to a non-empty string to set the PIN, or empty string to
    76→        skip setting.
    77→      selector:
    78→        template:
    79→    clear_on_end:

     1→---
     2→blueprint:
     3→  name: "Lock Code Manager: Calendar Condition"
     4→  description: >
     5→    Creates a binary sensor that turns ON when a calendar event is active and
     6→    an optional condition template is truthy. Assign the resulting binary sensor
     7→    as the condition entity for a Lock Code Manager code slot.
     8→
     9→
    10→    **Available template variables**
    11→
    12→    Your condition template can use these variables:
    13→
    14→    - `message` — calendar event summary/title
    15→
    16→    - `description` — calendar event description
    17→
    18→    - `location` — calendar event location
    19→
    20→    - `start_time` — event start time
    21→
    22→    - `end_time` — event end time
    23→
    24→    - `all_day` — whether the event is an all-day event
    25→
    26→    - `slot_number` — the resolved slot number
    27→
    28→    - `config_entry_title` — the LCM config entry title
    29→
    30→    - `name_state` — the current name of the code slot
    31→
    32→    - `lock_entity_ids` — list of lock entity IDs managed by this slot
    33→
    34→
    35→    **Example condition templates**
    36→
    37→    - Allow when event title contains "Guest": `{{ 'Guest' in message }}`
    38→
    39→    - Allow only for a specific lock: `{{ 'lock.front_door' in lock_entity_ids }}`
    40→
    41→
    42→    **Note:** The condition template and calendar attributes are evaluated when the
    43→    sensor is first loaded and when Home Assistant reloads template entities. For
    44→    the default `{{ true }}`, the sensor dynamically tracks the calendar on/off
    45→    state. For conditions that reference event attributes, reload template entities
    46→    after calendar event changes to re-evaluate.
    47→
    48→
    49→    **Template debugging**
    50→
    51→    To test your condition template, go to Developer Tools → Template and paste:

    56→
    57→    {% set description = state_attr('calendar.YOUR_CALENDAR', 'description') | default('', true) %}
    58→

    60→

    80→      selector:

    83→      name: Calendar entity
    84→      description: The calendar entity whose events control this condition.

    88→    condition_template:

{
  "file_path": "/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager.wiki/Blueprints.md",
  "content": "# Blueprints\n\nLock Code Manager provides Home Assistant blueprints for common automation patterns. Import them using the buttons below, or copy the YAML files from the [`blueprints/`](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/tree/main/blueprints) directory in the repository.\n\n## Slot Usage Limiter\n\n[![Import Blueprint](https://my.home-assistant.io/badges/blueprint_import.svg)](https://my.home-assistant.io/redirect/blueprint_import/?blueprint_url=https%3A%2F%2Fgithub.com%2Framan325%2Flock_code_manager%2Fblob%2Fmain%2Fblueprints%2Fautomation%2Flock_code_manager%2Fslot_usage_limiter.yaml)\n\n**Type:** Automation\n\nDecrements an `input_number` helper each time a code slot PIN is used. When the counter reaches 0, the slot is automatically disabled. Optionally resets the counter when the slot is re-enabled.\n\n### Setup\n\n1. Create an `input_number` helper (Settings → Devices & Services → Helpers) with min=0, max=your desired limit, and step=1.\n2. Import this blueprint using the button above.\n3. Create a new automation from the blueprint and configure it with your LCM config entry, slot number, and the helper you created.\n\n### Inputs\n\n| Input | Description | Default |\n|-------|-------------|---------|\n| **Lock Code Manager config entry** | The LCM config entry that manages your lock(s). | *(required)* |\n| **Code slot number** | The code slot number to monitor (1–999). | *(required)* |\n| **Uses counter** | An `input_number` helper that tracks remaining uses. | *(required)* |\n| **Initial uses on re-enable** | When > 0, the counter resets to this value each time the slot is re-enabled. Set to 0 to disable auto-reset. | `0` |\n\n> **Tip:** This blueprint provides an `input_number`-based alternative to the built-in [[Number of Uses|Using-\"Number-of-Uses\"]] feature. Use this if you want external visibility into the remaining count or need to programmatically reset it.\n\n---\n\n## Calendar Condition\n\n[![Import Blueprint](https://my.home-assistant.io/badges/blueprint_import.svg)](https://my.home-assistant.io/redirect/blueprint_import/?blueprint_url=https%3A%2F%2Fgithub.com%2Framan325%2Flock_code_manager%2Fblob%2Fmain%2Fblueprints%2Ftemplate%2Flock_code_manager%2Fcalendar_condition.yaml)\n\n**Type:** Template (binary sensor)\n\nCreates a binary sensor that turns ON when a calendar event is active and an optional condition template is truthy. Assign the resulting binary sensor as the [[condition entity|Using-Condition-Entities]] for a Lock Code Manager code slot.\n\n### Setup\n\n1. Import this blueprint using the button above.\n2. Configure it with your LCM config entry, slot number, calendar entity, and optionally a condition template.\n3. The blueprint creates a binary sensor — assign it as the condition entity for your code slot.\n\n### Inputs\n\n| Input | Description | Default |\n|-------|-------------|---------|\n| **Lock Code Manager config entry** | The LCM config entry that manages your lock(s). | *(required)* |\n| **Slot number** | The code slot number. Can be a static number or a Jinja2 template using calendar event attributes. | *(required)* |\n| **Calendar entity** | The calendar entity whose events control this condition. | *(required)* |\n| **Condition template** | A Jinja2 template that must evaluate to truthy for the sensor to turn ON (in addition to the calendar being active). | `{{ true }}` |\n\n### Available Template Variables\n\nYour condition template can use these variables:\n\n| Variable | Description |\n|----------|-------------|\n| `message` | Calendar event summary/title |\n| `description` | Calendar event description |\n| `location` | Calendar event location |\n| `start_time` | Event start time |\n| `end_time` | Event end time |\n| `all_day` | Whether the event is an all-day event |\n| `slot_number` | The resolved slot number |\n| `config_entry_title` | The LCM config entry title |\n| `name_state` | The current name of the code slot |\n| `lock_entity_ids` | List of lock entity IDs managed by this slot |\n\n### Example Condition Templates\n\n- Allow when event title contains \"Guest\": `{{ 'Guest' in message }}`\n- Allow only for a specific lock: `{{ 'lock.front_door' in lock_entity_ids }}`\n\n> **Note:** The condition template and calendar attributes are evaluated when the sensor is first loaded and when Home Assistant reloads template entities. For the default `{{ true }}`, the sensor dynamically tracks the calendar on/off state. For conditions that reference event attributes, reload template entities after calendar event changes to re-evaluate.\n\n---\n\n## Calendar PIN Setter\n\n[![Import Blueprint](https://my.home-assistant.io/badges/blueprint_import.svg)](https://my.home-assistant.io/redirect/blueprint_import/?blueprint_url=https%3A%2F%2Fgithub.com%2Framan325%2Flock_code_manager%2Fblob%2Fmain%2Fblueprints%2Fautomation%2Flock_code_manager%2Fcalendar_pin_setter.yaml)\n\n**Type:** Automation\n\nExtracts a PIN from calendar event attributes using a user-provided template and sets it on a Lock Code Manager code slot. Optionally clears the PIN when the calendar event ends.\n\n### Setup\n\n1. Import this blueprint using the button above.\n2. Create a new automation from the blueprint and configure it with your LCM config entry, calendar entity, slot number, and a PIN template.\n3. When a calendar event starts, the PIN is extracted and set on the slot. When it ends (if enabled), the PIN is cleared.\n\n### Inputs\n\n| Input | Description | Default |\n|-------|-------------|---------|\n| **Lock Code Manager config entry** | The LCM config entry that manages your lock(s). | *(required)* |\n| **Slot number** | The code slot number. Can be a static number or a Jinja2 template using calendar event attributes. | *(required)* |\n| **Calendar entity** | The calendar entity whose events provide PIN codes. | *(required)* |\n| **PIN template** | A Jinja2 template that extracts a PIN from calendar event attributes. Must evaluate to a non-empty string. | *(required)* |\n| **Clear PIN when event ends** | When enabled, the PIN is cleared when the calendar event ends. | `true` |\n\n### Available Template Variables\n\nYour PIN and slot number templates can use these variables:\n\n| Variable | Description |\n|----------|-------------|\n| `message` | Calendar event summary/title |\n| `description` | Calendar event description |\n| `location` | Calendar event location |\n| `start_time` | Event start time |\n| `end_time` | Event end time |\n| `all_day` | Whether the event is an all-day event |\n\n### Example PIN Templates\n\n- Extract a 4-digit PIN from description: `{{ description | regex_findall('\\b\\d{4}\\b') | first | default('') }}`\n- Use the first 4 characters of the event title: `{{ message[:4] if message | length >= 4 else '' }}`\n"
}

I've performed the web search and found relevant information about Home Assistant blueprints and custom integrations. Here's what the search revealed:

## Key Findings

**Blueprint Creation and Usage:**
Blueprints are created in YAML and can be used by adding them to configuration with use_blueprint directives. Currently, three types of blueprints are supported: automation, script and template.

**Programmatic Automation:**
The search results focus primarily on YAML-based blueprint configuration rather than programmatic creation from Python code. Integrations in Home Assistant are Python modules responsible for interacting with devices and services, translating their language into a standardized format Home Assistant understands.

**Custom Integration Blueprint (2025):**
A modern Home Assistant custom integration blueprint is designed to work with Home Assistant 2025.7+ and includes patterns and tooling needed to build professional integrations.

**Blueprint Schema:**
The blueprint configuration schema consists of 2 parts: the blueprint's high-level metadata and the schema for the blueprint domain it describes. Input fields can be provided using YAML definition or via a configuration form in the UI.

However, the search results don't provide specific information about programmatically creating automations from Python code within a custom integration. The documentation primarily covers YAML-based blueprint creation and usage through the UI. If you need more specific guidance on programmatic automation creation via Python, you may need to consult Home Assistant's Python API documentation or community resources.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

# Blueprint Websocket API Endpoints

Based on the code, here are the websocket API endpoints for blueprints:

1. **blueprint/list** - Lists available blueprints for a domain
   - Requires: `type`, `domain`
   - Returns metadata for each blueprint or error details

2. **blueprint/import** - Imports a blueprint from a URL
   - Requires: `type`, `url`
   - Returns: suggested filename, raw data, metadata, validation errors, and existence status

3. **blueprint/save** - Saves/creates a blueprint configuration
   - Requires: `type`, `domain`, `path`, `yaml`
   - Optional: `source_url`, `allow_override`
   - Returns: whether it overrides an existing file

4. **blueprint/delete** - Deletes an existing blueprint
   - Requires: `type`, `domain`, `path`

5. **blueprint/substitute** - "Process a blueprinted config to allow editing"
   - Requires: `type`, `domain`, `path`, `input`
   - Returns: substituted configuration

All endpoints require admin privileges via `@websocket_api.require_admin` decorator. The `blueprint/import` endpoint specifically handles creating automations from blueprints via imported URLs.

# Blueprint Automation Creation Process

Based on the code, automations are created from blueprints through this flow:

## Blueprint Detection and Input Processing

The code identifies blueprint instances via `blueprint.is_blueprint_instance_config(config)` and sets `uses_blueprint = True`. It then retrieves blueprints using `async_get_blueprints(hass)`.

## Key Processing Steps

1. **Input Extraction**: `blueprints.async_inputs_from_config()` extracts blueprint inputs from the configuration after applying backward compatibility schema transformations.

2. **Substitution**: `blueprint_inputs.async_substitute()` generates the actual automation configuration by substituting blueprint inputs into the template.

3. **Raw Config Capture**: The substituted configuration is stored as `raw_blueprint_inputs = blueprint_inputs.config_with_inputs`.

## Error Handling

When blueprint processing fails, the code either:
- Returns a minimal config with `ValidationStatus.FAILED_BLUEPRINT` status (if `raise_on_errors` is false)
- Raises `HomeAssistantError` (if `raise_on_errors` is true)

The resulting automation configuration object includes `raw_blueprint_inputs` to preserve the original blueprint input data alongside the validated automation configuration.
```

# Automation Creation/Update Analysis

## Service-Based Approach

The code shows automation management through **services** rather than direct WebSocket creation:

- **SERVICE_RELOAD**: `"Reload all automations and load new ones from config"` - allows reloading from configuration files
- **SERVICE_TRIGGER**: Manually triggers existing automations with variables
- **SERVICE_TURN_ON/OFF/TOGGLE**: Controls automation state

The reload service accepts an optional `CONF_ID` parameter to update a single automation: `{vol.Optional(CONF_ID): str}`

## Storage Mechanism

The code reveals automations are **configuration-based**, not programmatically created:

1. **Configuration File Driven**: Automations load from `config[DOMAIN]` configuration blocks containing triggers, conditions, and actions
2. **Blueprint Integration**: Automations can reference blueprints via `CONF_USE_BLUEPRINT` with a `CONF_PATH`
3. **Raw Config Storage**: Each automation entity stores `raw_config` and `raw_blueprint_inputs` for later retrieval

## WebSocket API

Only one WebSocket command exists for automations:

```
"automation/config" with "entity_id" parameter
```

This retrieves existing automation configuration but doesn't create new ones. The response provides the automation's `raw_config`.

## Conclusion

**No programmatic creation API exists in this codebase.** Automations must be created by modifying configuration files (YAML/JSON), then reloading via the `SERVICE_RELOAD` service. The system emphasizes configuration management over dynamic creation.
```

# Automation Config REST API Analysis

Based on the provided code, here's what I found:

## View Class
The `EditAutomationConfigView` class extends `EditIdBasedConfigView` and handles POST requests for creating/updating automations.

## Storage Location
The configurations are written to the file specified by `AUTOMATION_CONFIG_PATH`:

```python
hass.http.register_view(
    EditAutomationConfigView(
        AUTOMATION_DOMAIN,
        "config",
        AUTOMATION_CONFIG_PATH,
        ...
    )
)
```

The actual path constant is imported from `homeassistant.config` but not defined in this excerpt.

## Write Mechanism
The `_write_value()` method handles storing automation data. It:
- Ensures configs have IDs (generating UUIDs if missing)
- Maintains ordered keys: "alias", "description", "triggers", "trigger", "conditions", "condition", "actions", "action"
- Appends new configs or updates existing ones by matching config IDs

## Post-Write Hook
After saving, a hook function either reloads the automation service or removes the entity from the registry if deleted.

**Note:** The code doesn't explicitly show `use_blueprint` field handling, though the `updated_value.update(new_value)` line would preserve any extra fields present in the input.
```

> AGENT

Yes, it's feasible. There are two layers to it:

**1. Installing the blueprint itself** — HA stores blueprints in `{config_dir}/blueprints/{domain}/{source}/`. You can either:
- Copy the YAML file directly to the filesystem from your integration
- Use the `blueprint/save` websocket command (admin-only, takes `domain`, `path`, `yaml`)

**2. Creating an automation/template from the blueprint** — HA's config component exposes `EditAutomationConfigView` which accepts POST to `/api/config/automation/config/<id>`. The payload can include `use_blueprint`:

```python
# What an automation-from-blueprint config looks like
{
    "alias": "LCM: Slot 1 Usage Limiter",
    "use_blueprint": {
        "path": "lock_code_manager/slot_usage_limiter.yaml",
        "input": {
            "config_entry": config_entry.entry_id,
            "slot_number": 1,
            "uses_counter": "input_number.slot_1_uses",
            "initial_uses": 5,
        },
    },
}
```

From Python inside a custom integration, you'd likely:
1. Write the blueprint YAML to `hass.config.path("blueprints", domain, "lock_code_manager", filename)`
2. Write the automation config to `automations.yaml` via the config store helpers (or call the internal view)
3. Call `hass.services.async_call("automation", "reload")`

For template blueprints (Calendar Condition → binary sensor), it's the same pattern but with the `template` domain and its corresponding config path.

The migration flow you're describing — detect `number_of_uses` in the config entry, offer to create a Slot Usage Limiter blueprint instance, then remove the built-in feature — makes sense architecturally. The main complexity would be creating the `input_number` helper programmatically too (via `hass.helpers` or the `input_number` component's config store).

Worth investigating the exact internal APIs closer when you start that PR, since these are internal HA APIs that aren't publicly documented and could change.

Sources:
- [HA Blueprint WebSocket API](https://github.com/home-assistant/core/tree/dev/homeassistant/components/blueprint)
- [HA Automation Config REST API](https://github.com/home-assistant/core/tree/dev/homeassistant/components/config)
- [REST API Developer Docs](https://developers.home-assistant.io/docs/api/rest/)
- [Using Automation Blueprints](https://www.home-assistant.io/docs/automation/using_blueprints/)

Please document this requirement (and/or add a guard that surfaces a clear message when no supported locks are present).\n```suggestion\n\n    **Requirements**\n\n    This blueprint relies on the `event.*_code_slot_*_pin_used` entity firing,\n    which only happens for locks/providers that support code slot events.\n    At least one such lock must be present and managed by this Lock Code\n    Manager config entry; otherwise, the counter will never decrement and this\n    automation will effectively do nothing.\n\n    In this repository, `VirtualLock.supports_code_slot_events` is `False`, so\n    virtual locks do **not** expose `*_code_slot_*_pin_used` events. Perfect! I have all the information needed. Let me provide you with a clear answer.

## Answer

The `event_types` attribute of the `pin_used` event entity contains **lock entity IDs**, not event type strings.

Here's the evidence from the codebase:

### The Implementation

From `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/event.py` (lines 82-84):

```python
@property
def event_types(self) -> list[str]:
    """Return supported event types (lock entity IDs)."""
    return [lock.lock.entity_id for lock in self._get_supported_locks()]
```

The `event_types` property explicitly returns a list of **lock entity IDs** by extracting `lock.lock.entity_id` from each supported lock.

### Supporting Evidence from Tests

The test file `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/tests/test_event.py` confirms this in the test `test_event_types_are_lock_entity_ids` (lines 72-84):

```python
async def test_event_types_are_lock_entity_ids(
    hass: HomeAssistant,
    mock_lock_config_entry,
    lock_code_manager_config_entry,
):
    """Test that event_types are the lock entity IDs that support code slot events."""
    state = hass.states.get(SLOT_1_EVENT_ENTITY)
    assert state

    # event_types should include lock entity IDs
    event_types = state.attributes.get("event_types", [])
    assert LOCK_1_ENTITY_ID in event_types
    assert LOCK_2_ENTITY_ID in event_types
```

### Summary

So when you call `state_attr(event_entity, 'event_types')`, it returns a list of **lock entity IDs** (like `"lock.front_door"`, `"lock.back_door"`, etc.) - specifically, those locks that support code slot events. These are NOT generic event type strings like `"pin_used"` or similar.

All done. Summary:

**Addressed (committed + pushed):**
- **#3**: Added Requirements note to slot_usage_limiter documenting code slot event support is needed
- **#7**: Clarified `lock_entity_ids` only includes locks with code slot event support

**Dismissed (replied on PR):**
- **#1, #4, #6**: `integration_entities(title)` is intentional — scopes to selected config entry, not all LCM entries
- **#2**: HA handles empty template entity_ids gracefully; `default('')` is standard practice
- **#5**: Template debugging section already covers diagnosis; adding notifications would overcomplicate v1

> AGENT

It looks like `home-assistant-native` wasn't actually in `.claude.json` — it must have been coming from the desktop app's remote MCP connection. Since you've removed it, that's sorted.

The HA native MCP integration requires OAuth-based auth that the desktop app's "Add Integration" flow may not handle correctly for local HA instances. If you want it in the desktop app, you'd need to configure it via the JSON config file with the right auth token rather than through the UI connect flow. But since the addon server covers the same functionality, it's probably not needed.

> DEVELOPER

I was able to do oauth using claude code by forcing the callback port

> AGENT

Good to know — so the desktop app's OAuth flow just doesn't handle the callback correctly, but Claude Code works when you force the port. That makes sense since Claude Code gives you more control over the auth flow.

> AGENT

Good — all loggers use `__name__`, so `custom_components.lock_code_manager` is the root logger. Now, before I start building this, there are a couple of design decisions where your input would shape things:

**1. Wiki page scope** — Two approaches:
- **Focused**: Just "How to File a Bug Report" — debug logs, diagnostic info, what to include
- **Broader**: "Troubleshooting" page that covers debug logs, common issues, AND has a section on filing a quality bug report

**2. Issue template update** — The current `bug_report.yml` asks for logs but doesn't explain how. Options:
- **Link approach**: Add a markdown block linking to the wiki page for instructions (keeps template lean)
- **Inline approach**: Embed the `configuration.yaml` snippet directly in the template description (self-contained but longer)
- **Both**: Brief inline hint + wiki link for details

**3. Diagnostic info** — Should the template ask for a dump of LCM entity states (e.g., from Developer Tools > States filtered to `lock_code_manager`)? This would help diagnose state-related bugs but adds friction for reporters.

What's your preference on these? I'm leaning toward a focused wiki page + link approach in the template, but you know your issue reporters better.

{
  "file_path": "/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager.wiki/Troubleshooting.md",
  "content": "# Troubleshooting\n\nThis section helps you diagnose issues with Lock Code Manager and file effective bug reports.\n\n## Before Filing an Issue\n\n1. **Check the wiki** — Many questions are answered in the [[Features|Home#features]] documentation.\n2. **Update to the latest version** — Your issue may already be fixed. Check [releases](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/releases) for recent changes.\n3. **Reproduce with debug logging** — Enable debug logs *before* reproducing the issue so you capture the full trace. See [[Troubleshooting: Debug Logs|Troubleshooting-Debug-Logs]].\n4. **Gather device diagnostics** — Download diagnostics for your lock device so maintainers can see exactly what HA knows about it. See [[Troubleshooting: Device Diagnostics|Troubleshooting-Device-Diagnostics]].\n\n## Filing a Bug Report\n\nUse the [bug report template](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/issues/new?template=bug_report.yml) to file an issue. A good bug report includes:\n\n- **Clear description** of what happened vs. what you expected\n- **Steps to reproduce** the issue\n- **Debug logs** captured while reproducing (see [[Troubleshooting: Debug Logs|Troubleshooting-Debug-Logs]])\n- **Device diagnostics** for your lock (see [[Troubleshooting: Device Diagnostics|Troubleshooting-Device-Diagnostics]])\n- **HA and LCM versions**\n\n> **Note:** These troubleshooting pages focus on the backend integration. Frontend/dashboard issues (cards not rendering, strategy errors, etc.) may require browser console logs instead — mention this in your issue if applicable.\n\n## Subpages\n\n- [[Troubleshooting: Debug Logs|Troubleshooting-Debug-Logs]] — How to enable debug logging for LCM\n- [[Troubleshooting: Device Diagnostics|Troubleshooting-Device-Diagnostics]] — How to download diagnostics for your lock device\n"

{
  "file_path": "/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager.wiki/Troubleshooting-Debug-Logs.md",
  "content": "# Troubleshooting: Debug Logs\n\nDebug logs capture the detailed decision-making inside Lock Code Manager — when codes are set/cleared, why a slot was enabled/disabled, and how entity states are evaluated. They are essential for diagnosing issues.\n\n## Enabling Debug Logging\n\nAdd the following to your `configuration.yaml` and restart Home Assistant:\n\n```yaml\nlogger:\n  default: warning\n  logs:\n    custom_components.lock_code_manager: debug\n```\n\nThis enables debug logging for all LCM modules (config flow, providers, entity platforms, etc.).\n\n## Capturing Logs for a Bug Report\n\n1. Enable debug logging as shown above and restart HA.\n2. Reproduce the issue.\n3. Go to **Settings → System → Logs** and click **Load full logs**.\n4. Filter or search for `lock_code_manager`.\n5. Copy the relevant log lines into your bug report.\n\n> **Tip:** You can also download the full log file from **Settings → System → Logs → Download full log**. This is helpful for intermittent issues where you need the complete timeline.\n\n## Temporary Debug Logging (No Restart)\n\nIf you don't want to edit `configuration.yaml`, you can enable debug logging temporarily via **Developer Tools → Services**:\n\n**Service:** `logger.set_level`\n\n```yaml\ncustom_components.lock_code_manager: debug\n```\n\nThis resets when HA restarts.\n\n## What to Include in a Bug Report\n\n- All `lock_code_manager` log lines from **before** the issue occurs through the issue itself. Context leading up to the problem is often more useful than the error alone.\n- If the logs are long, attach them as a file rather than pasting inline.\n- **Do not redact entity IDs or state values** unless they contain sensitive information (like PINs). Entity names and states are critical for diagnosis.\n- **Do redact PINs** if they appear in the logs.\n\n## Lock Integration Logs\n\nIf the issue involves codes not being set on the lock itself, you may also want to enable debug logging for your lock's integration:\n\n```yaml\nlogger:\n  default: warning\n  logs:\n    custom_components.lock_code_manager: debug\n    # Add your lock integration's logger, e.g.:\n    homeassistant.components.zwave_js: debug\n```\n\n[[← Back to Troubleshooting|Troubleshooting]]\n"
}
```

{
  "file_path": "/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager.wiki/Troubleshooting-Device-Diagnostics.md",
  "content": "# Troubleshooting: Device Diagnostics\n\nDevice diagnostics provide a snapshot of everything Home Assistant knows about your lock — its capabilities, supported features, entity registry entries, and current state. This helps maintainers understand your lock's setup without needing access to your system.\n\n> **Important:** The diagnostics download comes from your **lock's integration** (e.g., Z-Wave JS), not from Lock Code Manager itself. LCM manages codes on locks provided by other integrations, so we need to see what those integrations expose.\n\n## Downloading Device Diagnostics\n\n1. Go to **Settings → Devices & Services**.\n2. Find the integration that provides your lock (e.g., **Z-Wave JS**).\n3. Click on the integration, then click on your **lock device**.\n4. Click the three-dot menu (⋮) in the top-right corner.\n5. Select **Download diagnostics**.\n6. Attach the downloaded JSON file to your bug report.\n\n> **Note:** HA automatically redacts sensitive information in diagnostics downloads. If you're concerned about any remaining data, review the JSON before uploading.\n\n## What Diagnostics Tell Us\n\nThe diagnostics file helps us understand:\n\n- **Device capabilities** — What features the lock reports (user codes, code slots, etc.)\n- **Entity details** — Which entities exist for the device and their configurations\n- **State values** — Current states that may be relevant to the issue\n- **Integration-specific data** — Protocol-level details (e.g., Z-Wave command classes, node configuration)\n\n## Multiple Locks\n\nIf you manage multiple locks with LCM and the issue is specific to one lock, download diagnostics for the affected lock. If the issue involves synchronization across locks, download diagnostics for all of them.\n\n[[← Back to Troubleshooting|Troubleshooting]]\n"
}
```

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The diagnostics field uses `render: json` so GitHub auto-formats the pasted JSON in a collapsible code block — keeps issues readable even with large dumps.
- The "both" approach means the template works for someone who just follows inline instructions, while the wiki links provide deeper context for those who want it. The wiki pages also serve as standalone troubleshooting docs outside the issue-filing context.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

Based on the search results, here's what I found about GitHub issue forms YAML file upload input type:

## Upload Input Type

GitHub issue forms support an upload element that allows you to add a file upload field to your form, where contributors can drag and drop files or click to browse and select files directly within the form.

## Basic Syntax Example

Here's how to use the upload input type in your YAML configuration:

An example showing the upload type with id "screenshots" includes attributes like label "Upload screenshots" with description "If applicable, add screenshots to help explain your problem" and validation for whether the field is required.

## Supported Input Types

GitHub issue forms support several field types, including text inputs, dropdowns, checkboxes, and file uploads.

## Note on File Attachments

While the upload element is available, contributors can also attach files in textarea fields, providing an alternative way to include file uploads in your issue forms if needed.

The upload input type follows the same YAML structure as other form elements, with a `type: upload` declaration, an `id` for reference, and `attributes` section for configuration options like `label`, `description`, and `validations`.


REMINDER: You MUST include the sources above in your response to the user using markdown hyperlinks.

# GitHub Issue Form Input Types

Yes, GitHub's form schema supports a file upload type. Here are all six supported form element types:

1. **markdown** - Displays Markdown text (not submitted)
2. **textarea** - Multi-line text field
3. **input** - Single-line text field
4. **dropdown** - Dropdown menu selection
5. **checkboxes** - Set of checkboxes
6. **upload** - File upload field

As the documentation states: "You can choose from the following types of form elements. Each type has unique attributes and validations." The upload element allows contributors to "drag and drop files or click to browse and select files directly within the form," supporting archives, documents, images, videos, and text files up to specific size limits.
```

Done.

- **PR**: https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/pull/918
- **Wiki**: Pushed — Troubleshooting pages and all blueprint content are live

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
- The `type: upload` element is relatively new in GitHub issue forms — most templates still use `textarea` for file attachments. Upload gives a proper drag-and-drop zone and avoids users accidentally pasting truncated JSON.
- Wiki pages are pushed directly to `master` on the wiki repo (GitHub wikis don't support branches/PRs), so they're live immediately. The issue template change goes through a PR since it's in the main repo.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

```
     1→# Troubleshooting: Debug Logs
     2→
     3→Debug logs capture the detailed decision-making inside Lock Code Manager — when codes are set/cleared, why a slot was enabled/disabled, and how entity states are evaluated. They are essential for diagnosing issues.
     4→
     5→## Enabling Debug Logging
     6→
     7→Add the following to your `configuration.yaml` and restart Home Assistant:
     8→
     9→```yaml
    10→logger:
    11→  default: warning
    12→  logs:
    13→    custom_components.lock_code_manager: debug
    14→```
    15→
    16→This enables debug logging for all LCM modules (config flow, providers, entity platforms, etc.).
    17→
    18→## Capturing Logs for a Bug Report
    19→
    20→1. Enable debug logging as shown above and restart HA.
    21→2. Reproduce the issue.
    22→3. Go to **Settings → System → Logs** and click **Load full logs**.
    23→4. Filter or search for `lock_code_manager`.
    24→5. Copy the relevant log lines into your bug report.
    25→
    26→> **Tip:** You can also download the full log file from **Settings → System → Logs → Download full log**. This is helpful for intermittent issues where you need the complete timeline.
    27→
    28→## Temporary Debug Logging (No Restart)
    29→
    30→If you don't want to edit `configuration.yaml`, you can enable debug logging temporarily via **Developer Tools → Services**:
    31→
    32→**Service:** `logger.set_level`
    33→
    34→```yaml
    35→custom_components.lock_code_manager: debug
    36→```
    37→
    38→This resets when HA restarts.
    39→
    40→## What to Include in a Bug Report
    41→
    42→- All `lock_code_manager` log lines from **before** the issue occurs through the issue itself. Context leading up to the problem is often more useful than the error alone.
    43→- If the logs are long, attach them as a file rather than pasting inline.
    44→- **Do not redact entity IDs or state values** unless they contain sensitive information (like PINs). Entity names and states are critical for diagnosis.
    45→- **Do redact PINs** if they appear in the logs.
    46→
    47→## Lock Integration Logs
    48→
    49→If the issue involves codes not being set on the lock itself, you may also want to enable debug logging for your lock's integration:
    50→
    51→```yaml
    52→logger:
    53→  default: warning
    54→  logs:
    55→    custom_components.lock_code_manager: debug
    56→    # Add your lock integration's logger, e.g.:
    57→    homeassistant.components.zwave_js: debug
    58→```
    59→
    60→[[← Back to Troubleshooting|Troubleshooting]]
    61→
```

  "file_path": "/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager.wiki/Troubleshooting-Debug-Logs.md",
  "content": "# Troubleshooting: Debug Logs\n\nDebug logs capture the detailed decision-making inside Lock Code Manager — when codes are set/cleared, why a slot was enabled/disabled, and how entity states are evaluated. They are essential for diagnosing issues.\n\n## Enabling Debug Logging\n\nAdd the following to your `configuration.yaml` and restart Home Assistant:\n\n```yaml\nlogger:\n  default: warning\n  logs:\n    custom_components.lock_code_manager: debug\n    homeassistant.components.zwave_js: debug\n```\n\n> **Note:** If your lock uses a different integration (not Z-Wave JS), replace `homeassistant.components.zwave_js` with the appropriate logger for your lock's integration.\n\nThis enables debug logging for both LCM (config flow, providers, entity platforms, etc.) and the lock integration so you can see the full picture of what's happening on both sides.\n\n## Capturing Logs for a Bug Report\n\n1. Enable debug logging as shown above and restart HA.\n2. Reproduce the issue.\n3. Go to **Settings → System → Logs** and click **Load full logs**.\n4. Filter or search for `lock_code_manager` and your lock integration.\n5. Copy the relevant log lines into your bug report, or download the full log file.\n\n> **Tip:** You can download the full log file from **Settings → System → Logs → Download full log**. This is helpful for intermittent issues where you need the complete timeline, and avoids GitHub's paste size limits.\n\n## Temporary Debug Logging (No Restart)\n\nIf you don't want to edit `configuration.yaml`, you can enable debug logging temporarily via **Developer Tools → Services**:\n\n**Service:** `logger.set_level`\n\n```yaml\ncustom_components.lock_code_manager: debug\nhomeassistant.components.zwave_js: debug\n```\n\nThis resets when HA restarts.\n\n## What to Include in a Bug Report\n\n- All `lock_code_manager` and lock integration log lines from **before** the issue occurs through the issue itself. Context leading up to the problem is often more useful than the error alone.\n- If the logs are long, upload them as a file rather than pasting inline.\n- **Do not redact entity IDs or state values** unless they contain sensitive information (like PINs). Entity names and states are critical for diagnosis.\n- **Do redact PINs** if they appear in the logs.\n\n[[← Back to Troubleshooting|Troubleshooting]]\n"
}
```

     1→# Troubleshooting: Debug Logs
     2→
     3→Debug logs capture the detailed decision-making inside Lock Code Manager — when codes are set/cleared, why a slot was enabled/disabled, and how entity states are evaluated. They are essential for diagnosing issues.
     4→
     5→## Enabling Debug Logging
     6→
     7→Add the following to your `configuration.yaml` and restart Home Assistant:
     8→
     9→```yaml
    10→logger:
    11→  default: warning
    12→  logs:
    13→    custom_components.lock_code_manager: debug
    14→    homeassistant.components.zwave_js: debug
    15→```
    16→
    17→> **Note:** If your lock uses a different integration, replace `zwave_js` with your lock integration's domain (e.g., `homeassistant.components.mqtt` for MQTT).
    18→
    19→This enables debug logging for both LCM (config flow, providers, entity platforms, etc.) and the lock integration so you can see the full picture of what's happening on both sides.
    20→
    21→## Capturing Logs for a Bug Report
    22→
    23→1. Enable debug logging as shown above and restart HA.
    24→2. Reproduce the issue.
    25→3. Go to **Settings → System → Logs** and click **Load full logs**.
    26→4. Filter or search for `lock_code_manager` and your lock integration.
    27→5. Copy the relevant log lines into your bug report, or download the full log file.
    28→
    29→> **Tip:** You can download the full log file from **Settings → System → Logs → Download full log**. This is helpful for intermittent issues where you need the complete timeline, and avoids GitHub's paste size limits.
    30→
    31→## Temporary Debug Logging (No Restart)
    32→
    33→If you don't want to edit `configuration.yaml`, you can enable debug logging temporarily via **Developer Tools → Services**:
    34→
    35→**Service:** `logger.set_level`
    36→
    37→```yaml
    38→custom_components.lock_code_manager: debug
    39→homeassistant.components.zwave_js: debug
    40→```
    41→
    42→This resets when HA restarts.
    43→
    44→## What to Include in a Bug Report
    45→
    46→- All `lock_code_manager` and lock integration log lines from **before** the issue occurs through the issue itself. Context leading up to the problem is often more useful than the error alone.
    47→- If the logs are long, upload them as a file rather than pasting inline.
    48→- **Do not redact entity IDs or state values** unless they contain sensitive information (like PINs). Entity names and states are critical for diagnosis.
    49→- **Do redact PINs** if they appear in the logs.

    21→## Capturing Logs for a Bug Report
    22→
    23→1. Enable debug logging as shown above and restart HA.
    24→2. Reproduce the issue.
    25→3. Go to **Settings → System → Logs** and click **Load full logs**.
    26→4. Filter or search for `lock_code_manager` and your lock integration.
    27→5. Copy the relevant log lines into your bug report, or download the full log file.
    28→
    29→> **Tip:** You can download the full log file from **Settings → System → Logs → Download full log**. This is helpful for intermittent issues where you need the complete timeline, and avoids GitHub's paste size limits.
    30→
    31→## Temporary Debug Logging (No Restart)
    32→
    33→If you don't want to edit `configuration.yaml`, you can enable debug logging temporarily via **Developer Tools → Services**:
    34→
    35→**Service:** `logger.set_level`
```

     1→# Troubleshooting
     2→
     3→This section helps you diagnose issues with Lock Code Manager and file effective bug reports.
     4→
     5→## Before Filing an Issue
     6→
     7→1. **Check the wiki** — Many questions are answered in the [[Features|Home#features]] documentation.
     8→2. **Update to the latest version** — Your issue may already be fixed. Check [releases](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/releases) for recent changes.
     9→3. **Reproduce with debug logging** — Enable debug logs *before* reproducing the issue so you capture the full trace. See [[Troubleshooting: Debug Logs|Troubleshooting-Debug-Logs]].
    10→4. **Gather device diagnostics** — Download diagnostics for your lock device so maintainers can see exactly what HA knows about it. See [[Troubleshooting: Device Diagnostics|Troubleshooting-Device-Diagnostics]].
    11→
    12→## Filing a Bug Report
    13→
    14→Use the [bug report template](https://github.com/raman325/lock_code_manager/issues/new?template=bug_report.yml) to file an issue. A good bug report includes:
    15→
    16→- **Clear description** of what happened vs. what you expected
    17→- **Steps to reproduce** the issue
    18→- **Debug logs** captured while reproducing (see [[Troubleshooting: Debug Logs|Troubleshooting-Debug-Logs]])
    19→- **Device diagnostics** for your lock (see [[Troubleshooting: Device Diagnostics|Troubleshooting-Device-Diagnostics]])
    20→- **HA and LCM versions**
    21→
    22→> **Note:** These troubleshooting pages focus on the backend integration. Frontend/dashboard issues (cards not rendering, strategy errors, etc.) may require browser console logs instead — mention this in your issue if applicable.
    23→
    24→## Subpages
    25→
    26→- [[Troubleshooting: Debug Logs|Troubleshooting-Debug-Logs]] — How to enable debug logging for LCM
    27→- [[Troubleshooting: Device Diagnostics|Troubleshooting-Device-Diagnostics]] — How to download diagnostics for your lock device
    28→
```

```json
{
  "file_path": "/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager.wiki/Troubleshooting-Browser-Console-Logs.md",
  "content": "# Troubleshooting: Browser Console Logs\n\nBrowser console logs capture errors and warnings from LCM's frontend components — dashboard strategies, custom cards, and Lovelace rendering. They are essential for diagnosing UI issues.\n\n## Opening the Browser Console\n\n### Chrome / Edge\n\n1. Open your Home Assistant dashboard in the browser.\n2. Press **F12** (or **Ctrl+Shift+J** on Windows/Linux, **Cmd+Option+J** on Mac).\n3. Click the **Console** tab.\n\n### Firefox\n\n1. Open your Home Assistant dashboard in the browser.\n2. Press **F12** (or **Ctrl+Shift+K** on Windows/Linux, **Cmd+Option+K** on Mac).\n3. Click the **Console** tab.\n\n### Safari\n\n1. Enable the developer menu: **Safari → Settings → Advanced → Show Develop menu**.\n2. Open your Home Assistant dashboard.\n3. Press **Cmd+Option+C** or go to **Develop → Show JavaScript Console**.\n\n### Home Assistant Companion App (Mobile)\n\nThe companion app does not expose a browser console directly. To capture frontend logs on mobile:\n\n1. Open your HA instance in a mobile browser (Chrome, Safari) instead of the companion app.\n2. For **Android**: connect the device via USB, open `chrome://inspect` on your desktop Chrome, and use remote debugging.\n3. For **iOS**: connect the device via USB, open Safari on your Mac, and use **Develop → [device name] → [HA tab]**.\n\n## Capturing Console Logs for a Bug Report\n\n1. Open the browser console **before** reproducing the issue.\n2. Clear the console (click the 🚫 icon or type `clear()`) to remove old messages.\n3. Reproduce the issue.\n4. Look for red (errors) and yellow (warnings) messages. Include **all** of them — don't try to pick out which ones seem relevant.\n5. Right-click in the console and select **Save as...** to download the full console log, or copy and paste the messages.\n\n> **Tip:** Share the full console output rather than individual error lines. Like backend logs, surrounding context matters — an earlier warning may explain a later error.\n\n## Common Frontend Issues\n\nThese are the types of issues where console logs are needed:\n\n- Cards not rendering or showing errors\n- Dashboard strategy not generating views\n- Missing or incorrect entity data in the UI\n- JavaScript errors after updating LCM or HA\n\n## What to Include in a Bug Report\n\n- The **full console output** from before reproducing through the issue\n- Your **browser name and version** (e.g., Chrome 130, Safari 18)\n- Whether you're using the **companion app** or a browser\n- A **screenshot** of the issue alongside the console logs\n\n[[← Back to Troubleshooting|Troubleshooting]]\n"
}
```

> AGENT

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
The path filtering itself is working correctly — `Python` is skipped because PR #914 only touches `package.json` and `yarn.lock`, which are frontend paths. The `Python` job correctly requires `**.py`, `requirements*.txt`, or `pyproject.toml` changes to run. So the skip is intentional behavior, not a bug.
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

The CI is actually passing — `Python` was correctly skipped since the PR only changes `package.json` and `yarn.lock`. All the checks that ran (Frontend, HACS, Hassfest, etc.) passed.

What was the failure you saw? Was it a specific run that failed earlier, or is the issue that `Python: skipping` is showing as a required check that blocks merge? If it's the latter, that's a GitHub branch protection issue — skipped jobs from path filtering need to not be required, or you need a "gate" job pattern.

{"body":"## Proposed change\n\nWhen a lock rejects a code because it duplicates a code in another slot, Z-Wave sends an Access Control notification (type 6, event 15: `NEW_USER_CODE_NOT_ADDED_DUE_TO_DUPLICATE_CODE`). Previously, LCM ignored this notification, so the in-sync sensor would see the code isn't set, retry, get rejected again, and loop indefinitely.\n\nThis PR adds handling for this notification: when LCM initiated the set operation and receives event 15, it disables the affected slot in all matching config entries, logs an error, and creates a single lock-specific persistent notification listing all affected config entries so the user knows to set a unique code and re-enable the slot.\n\nKey design decisions:\n- **Guard against external triggers**: Only reacts when `_set_in_progress_code_slot` is set (i.e., LCM initiated the set), preventing false positives from codes added at the lock's keypad\n- **Lock-specific notification**: One persistent notification per lock+slot (not per config entry), listing all affected config entries in the message\n- **Field lifecycle**: `_set_in_progress_code_slot` is set before the service call, cleared on success (push value update) or failure (duplicate notification)\n\n## Type of change\n\n- [x] Bugfix (non-breaking change which fixes an issue)\n\n## Additional information\n\n- This PR fixes or closes issue: fixes #848\n\n🤖 Generated with [Claude Code](https://claude.com/claude-code)","title":"Handle duplicate code Z-Wave notification"}

{"body":"## Proposed change\n\nFix sync loops affecting Z-Wave locks using User Code Command Class V1 (e.g., Yale P-KFCON-MOD-YALE). V1 locks don't reliably update the Z-Wave JS value cache after set/clear operations. When `async_request_refresh()` fires after the optimistic `push_update()`, it reads stale cache data and overwrites the correct state, triggering a retry loop.\n\n### Fix\n\nAfter set/clear on V1 locks, call `get_usercode_from_node()` to poll the specific slot directly from the device. This force-updates the Z-Wave JS value cache before the coordinator reads it, so all downstream reads return correct data and no sync loop occurs.\n\n### Changes\n\n- **Poll slot after set/clear for V1** (`zwave_js.py`) — detect User Code CC version via `node.command_classes`; for V1, call `get_usercode_from_node()` after set/clear to update the cache\n- **Enforce D213 docstring rule** (`pyproject.toml`) — enable ruff D213 and fix all 68 existing violations\n\n## Type of change\n\n- [x] Bugfix (non-breaking change which fixes an issue)\n- [ ] Code quality improvement\n\n## Additional information\n\n- 253 tests pass, 3 new tests added covering V1 set/clear polling and V2 no-poll behavior\n- Tested against device diagnostics from a real Yale V1 lock (Node 136, firmware 33.16, User Code CC V1, no Supervision CC)\n\n🤖 Generated with [Claude Code](https://claude.com/claude-code)","title":"Fix V1 sync loop by polling slot after set/clear"}

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
There's a clear history here: PR #873 fixed V1 sync loops from stale cache, PR #863 fixed loops from stale AVAILABLE events, and PR #899 specifically handles the **duplicate code rejection** (Z-Wave event 15: `NEW_USER_CODE_NOT_ADDED_DUE_TO_DUPLICATE_CODE`). So the duplicate PIN scenario is already handled in the code — LCM disables the slot and sends a persistent notification.
`──────��────────────────────────��─────────────────`

This is great feedback from the user and confirms what PR #899 addressed. Two distinct issues they hit:

**1. Slots filled outside LCM's managed range** — LCM only manages the slots it's configured for. If other slots have codes (set manually at the keypad, or leftover from a previous system), LCM doesn't know about them. This isn't a bug per se, but it's a source of confusion.

**2. Duplicate code rejection** — Many locks (especially Z-Wave) reject setting a PIN if it already exists in another slot. PR #899 added handling for this — when Z-Wave sends event 15 (`NEW_USER_CODE_NOT_ADDED_DUE_TO_DUPLICATE_CODE`), LCM now disables the slot and shows a persistent notification. Before that PR, this caused an infinite retry loop.

The user's experience sounds like they were hitting both: stale codes in unmanaged slots that happened to duplicate LCM-managed PINs, causing silent set failures that looped forever.

A couple of things worth considering:

- **Is the user on the latest version?** If they're on a version before #899, they wouldn't have the duplicate code handling. The fix would be to update.
- **Should LCM document/warn about pre-existing codes?** A troubleshooting wiki entry or setup note about clearing unused slots before configuring LCM could prevent this. The wiki could mention using Developer Tools → Actions → `zwave_js.set_usercode` / `zwave_js.clear_usercode` to clean up.

## Summary of Findings

Based on my research, here are the answers to your four questions:

### 1. **Does LCM see ALL slots on the lock, or only the ones it manages?**

LCM **only sees and manages the configured slots**. Here's how:

**File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/zwave_js.py` (lines 684-738)

In `async_get_usercodes()`:
- Lines 687-690: It retrieves the list of code slots **only** from the config entries that manage this lock:
  ```python
  code_slots = {
      int(code_slot)
      for entry in self.hass.config_entries.async_entries(DOMAIN)
      for code_slot in get_entry_data(entry, CONF_SLOTS, {})
      if self.lock.entity_id in get_entry_data(entry, CONF_LOCKS, [])
  }
  ```
- Lines 708-727: It then filters the cached Z-Wave values to only include these managed slots, and returns only those in the data dictionary.

**Push updates:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/zwave_js.py` (lines 272-389)

Push updates are also filtered to only process the specific code slots that are managed. Lines 295-301 show it returns early for slot 0 (which is metadata, not a user slot) and only processes updates relevant to the managed configuration.

**Polling:** The coordinator in `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/coordinator.py` calls `async_get_usercodes()` which returns only managed slots. Line 43 shows polling is disabled when push is supported.

---

### 2. **Where are codes set on the lock?**

There are two paths:

**Path A - Configuration Entity Changes (Normal Flow):**
1. User changes PIN text entity or enabled switch
2. **File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/text.py` (line 99) or `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/switch.py` (lines 77, 81)
   - Calls `self._update_config_entry(value)` which updates config entry data
3. **File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/__init__.py` (lines 405-645)
   - `async_update_listener` is triggered by config entry change
4. **File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/binary_sensor.py` (lines 502-542)
   - `LockCodeManagerCodeSlotInSyncEntity._perform_sync_operation()` compares config state vs coordinator data
   - Lines 509-512: Calls `await self.lock.async_internal_set_usercode()` when enabled and PIN differs
   - OR lines 521: Calls `await self.lock.async_internal_clear_usercode()` when disabled
5. **File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/zwave_js.py` (lines 579-627)
   - `async_set_usercode()` method:
   - Line 617: Sets `self._set_in_progress_code_slot = code_slot` to track the ongoing set
   - Lines 612-613: Calls the Z-Wave service:
     ```python
     await self.async_call_service(
         ZWAVE_JS_DOMAIN, SERVICE_SET_LOCK_USERCODE, service_data
     )
     ```
   - Lines 622-625: Optimistic update - calls `self.coordinator.push_update({code_slot: usercode})` to avoid sync loops

**Path B - WebSocket Command (Direct Set):**
1. **File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/websocket.py` (lines 1100-1149)
   - `set_lock_usercode()` function (lines 1133-1140)
   - Directly calls `await lock.async_internal_set_usercode()` or `await lock.async_internal_clear_usercode()`

---

### 3. **What does the duplicate code event handler from PR #899 do?**

**File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/zwave_js.py` (lines 478-540)

`_async_handle_duplicate_code()` flow:

1. **Event Detection** (lines 451-462): When Z-Wave event `NEW_USER_CODE_NOT_ADDED_DUE_TO_DUPLICATE_CODE` (AccessControl notification event 15) arrives:
   - Line 453: Only triggers if `self._set_in_progress_code_slot` is not None (LCM initiated the set)
   - Line 454: Checks if the event's userId matches 0 or the in-progress slot
   - Lines 458-462: Creates async task to handle it

2. **Handler Actions** (lines 479-540):
   - Line 482: Clears the `_set_in_progress_code_slot` tracking
   - Lines 486-498: Finds the config entry managing this lock/slot combination
   - Lines 501-508: Gets the "enabled" switch entity ID for this slot
   - Lines 510-516: **Turns off the switch** (`SERVICE_TURN_OFF`) to disable the slot (line 511)
   - Lines 518-540: Creates a persistent notification to the user with message:
     ```
     "Lock {lock_entity_id} rejected the code for slot {code_slot} (config entry {title}) 
      because it duplicates a code in another slot. Slot {code_slot} has been disabled. 
      Please set a unique code and re-enable slot {code_slot}"
     ```

---

### 4. **Is there any existing duplicate code detection before sending to the lock?**

**No, there is NO pre-flight duplicate code detection.** The system is reactive, not preventive:

1. **File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/providers/zwave_js.py` (lines 594-620)
   - `async_set_usercode()` only checks if the code is **already set to the same value** (lines 603-609) to avoid unnecessary network calls
   - No validation of whether the code duplicates another slot's code

2. The system relies on:
   - The **lock's native duplicate detection** (which sends the Z-Wave notification)
   - The **event handler (PR #899)** to react to that notification
   - User re-enabling and setting a unique code

3. **File:** `/Users/raman/projects/lock_code_manager/custom_components/lock_code_manager/binary_sensor.py` (lines 587-608)
   - The sync mechanism just calls the set operation and then refreshes coordinator data
   - No pre-check for duplicates

This is a lock-level concern - the Z-Wave lock firmware itself detects and rejects duplicates, and LCM handles the rejection gracefully by disabling the slot and notifying the user.
